It doesn't take minutes to peak, it doesn't take time to build. It's just an instant, overwhelming feeling right in my chest, right from the get-go. I don't have these... these episodes where it slowly creeps up on me and then peaks after a few minutes, no sir. It's just immediate. The door clicks, and my chest flips, and I just have to get out right then and there. So, I wouldn't say it peaks within minutes, because it doesn't even take that long. It's just right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: Well, a panic attack? I... I don't really know if I'd use those words, doc. I mean, when I hear \"panic attack,\" I think of folks on the television, you know, clutching their chests, gasping for air, falling out on the floor like they're having a heart attack or something. I saw a fella have one once down at the hardware store back in '98, maybe '99. He just went white as a sheet and started shaking all over, couldn't even stand up. They had to call the ambulance and everything. \n\nBut with me? In those elevators? It's not like that. I'm not falling on the floor. I'm not gasping for air like a fish out of water. It's just that sudden jolt, that flip in the chest, and the absolute, burning need to just get out of that space. I don't feel like I'm dying or losing my mind, I just know, plain as day, that I cannot be in that little box one second longer. So I turn around, I push the door open, I walk away. My heart might be beating a little fast, sure, but I'm not having a whole... episode, you know? I just need out, and once I'm out, I'm fine. So, no, I wouldn't call it a panic attack, not like what you see on TV or what that fella at the hardware store had. It's just... a strong reaction, I guess you could say. A really, really strong reaction.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid those elevators or small rooms because you're afraid of having one of those strong reactions?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I suppose that's a big part of it, doc. I mean, nobody wants to feel that way if they can help it, right? So, these days... well, I just work around it. Like with the elevators, I just take the stairs. It’s hard on the knees, sure, and my daughter fusses at me, says I’m gonna wear out my joints before my time, but I just tell her I need the exercise. It’s easier than trying to explain that I just can't stand the thought of those doors closing and being stuck in there, waiting for that awful feeling to hit.\n\nAnd it’s not just the elevators. I volunteer down at the historical museum—have for about twelve years now, ever since Martha passed—and sometimes they need folks to go down into the basement archives to pull old records. Now, those archive rooms, they’re tiny, no windows, heavy fire doors that latch shut behind you. I used to do it, but now... well, I just pass on those jobs. I tell 'em my eyesight isn't good enough for the dusty old print, or I let the younger volunteers handle it. \n\nIf there’s ever a time I absolutely can't avoid a closed-in place—like, say, a public restroom where the door has to lock and it’s real small—I’ll actually ask a friend to wait right outside. I’ll say, \"Hey, just stand right here, keep talking to me so I know you're there,\" which... well, I feel a bit foolish doing it, a man my age needing a buddy outside the bathroom door like a little kid. But it helps, knowing someone is right there on the other side. So, yeah, I avoid them when I can, or I find ways to deal with it if I can't, just to keep from having that... that reaction. I just don't want to feel that sudden urge to run, so I just steer clear.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of the small, enclosed spaces themselves, or is it more the feeling that something bad will happen to you when you're in them?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's an interesting question, really. I've spent a lot of time thinking about that myself, especially sitting out on the porch in the evenings. You know, you get to my age, and you have a lot of quiet time to just mull things over. And I've wondered, is it the room itself, or is it what might happen? \n\nI suppose... well, I suppose it's the space itself. It's not like I'm sitting there thinking, \"Oh, the ceiling is going to cave in on me,\" or \"I'm going to run out of air and suffocate.\" I don't have those thoughts. It's just... the confinement. The absolute, undeniable fact of being closed in. \n\nI know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up in a prison cell or something awful like that. I know that in my logical brain. I know there's a button to open the door, or a handle right there. But my mind... it just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's like my brain doesn't care that it's just the basement archive room where we keep the old land deeds from the 1800s. All my brain sees is a box with no way out. \n\nIt reminds me of this time, must have been back in... oh, maybe 1952? I was just a boy, and my older brother, Tommy—he was always a bit of a prankster, you know—he locked me in this old cedar chest my mother kept at the foot of the bed. Just for a minute, mind you, but I remember that feeling. That sudden realization that the lid was heavy and I couldn't push it up. I don't know if that has anything to do with it now, all these years later, but it's that same kind of feeling. It's not that I think the elevator cable is going to snap. It's just the simple fact that I am in a small space, the door is closed, and I can't just step outside if I want to. That's what gets me. The space itself, just closing in around me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to situations where you are physically enclosed, like elevators or small rooms?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's another thing I've turned over in my mind quite a bit. You see, when you start avoiding things—like those elevators, or the archive room down at the museum—you start noticing other things, too. You start paying attention to where you are and what's around you in a way you didn't used to. And I've thought about it, I really have. Is it just the small rooms? \n\nNow, I've got a nephew, my sister Sarah's boy, who lives out in California. He's terrified of flying. Won't get on a plane for love nor money. Says he feels trapped up there in the sky. And I thought to myself, \"Is that what I have? Am I afraid of airplanes?\" But, you know, I haven't been on an airplane since... oh, it must have been 1985, when Martha and I flew down to Florida for our anniversary. We went to see Epcot, you know, when it was still fairly new. And I didn't mind the flight at all. I mean, the seats were a bit cramped, and the food wasn't anything to write home about, but I didn't get that... that feeling. That urge to run. Of course, that was a long time ago, before this whole elevator business started getting so bad. \n\nAnd then there's driving. Some folks, I hear, they get nervous driving over bridges or through tunnels. Now, tunnels, I can see how that might bother some people. It's enclosed, sure. But we don't have many tunnels around here, not like out East. And bridges? Never bothered me a bit. I used to drive a delivery truck back in the day, hauling furniture all over the state, crossed a hundred bridges, never thought twice about it. \n\nEven crowds... you know, some people get antsy in a big crowd, like at a concert or a crowded mall. But I don't mind that so much. I mean, I don't like being jostled around, who does? But I don't feel trapped, because I can always see the door, you know? I can see the way out. \n\nSo, I guess... well, I guess I'm not entirely sure, doc. I haven't really noticed it happening anywhere else besides those small, closed-in rooms. The elevators, the closets, the bathrooms without windows... those are the places that really get to me. I suppose if I were to get on an airplane tomorrow, I might feel it, but I haven't tried, so I couldn't say for sure. As far as I know, it's just those physical, enclosed spaces right here on solid ground that give me that trouble. It's just... well, it's a peculiar thing, isn't it? How the mind works.\n\nDoctor: Is it the fact that you cannot escape that bothers you, or is it something about the small space itself that triggers your reaction?\n\nPatient: Well, now, that’s... that’s a mighty fine line you’re drawing there, doc. I mean, when you really get down to the brass tacks of it, aren’t they sort of two sides of the same old coin? I’ve sat on the porch and chewed on this kind of thing, like I said, but separating the two... I just don't know if I can rightly do that. \n\nYou see, when I look at a small space—like, say, that little half-bath over at my daughter's house, the one she had put in right under the stairs, which is just a toilet and a tiny sink and barely enough room for a man my size to turn around in—I see those walls, right? I see how close they are to my shoulders. And the very next second, my brain says, \"If that door shuts, you can't get out of here easy.\" \n\nSo, is it the walls being close, or is it the not getting out? I honestly couldn't tell you for sure. It’s a bit like asking if it’s the rain that gets you wet or the water in the rain, isn't it? I remember back in... oh, it must have been the winter of '98, we had this terrible ice storm that knocked the power out for nearly a week. Martha and I were cooped up in the living room with just the fireplace going to keep from freezing. We couldn't leave the house because the roads were a solid sheet of ice, completely trapped, but the house itself was big enough, so I didn't feel that panic. I didn't mind being stuck. \n\nBut you put me in a small room... well, they just go hand in hand, don't they? The space is small, which means I'm trapped, and I'm trapped because the space is small. I suppose if the room was small but somehow didn't have any walls, I wouldn't care. But I really just don't know how to give you a straight answer on that one, doc. I haven't been able to separate the two in my head. I haven't really noticed one being worse than the other, it's just... the whole kit and caboodle all at once that makes me want to turn tail and walk the other way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in small, enclosed spaces like elevators and rooms, or are there other situations that trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, like I was sort of touching on a minute ago with the driving and the crowds, I really haven't noticed anything else that gives me that... that particular flutter in my chest. I mean, I go about my day just fine otherwise. \n\nI walk down to the grocery store—used to be a Piggly Wiggly, but they bought it out and turned it into a Food Lion back in, oh, must have been 2006 or 2007—and I can walk down those aisles, even on a Tuesday morning when they're restocking the shelves and it's all cluttered up with cardboard boxes and carts in the way. Doesn't bother me a lick. I just say \"excuse me\" and squeeze right on past. \n\nAnd you know, some folks get spooked by all sorts of everyday things. My neighbor, Earl, he lives two houses down, he can't stand the sight of a snake. Even a little green garter snake in his tomato plants will have him hollering and running for the hills. But me? I'll just scoop it up with a snow shovel and toss it over the fence into the woods. Heights don't bother me neither. I used to clean my own gutters right up until my knees started giving out on me a few years back. My daughter Sarah, she'd come over and yell at me, saying, \"Dad, you're gonna fall and break your neck up there!\" But I was never scared of being up on that ladder, looking down. I just liked keeping the pine needles clear so the water wouldn't back up into the eaves, you know? \n\nSo when I really sit down and take inventory of it all, like I do on the porch... no, I can't rightly say there's anything else. No dogs, no thunderstorms, no heights. Just those little boxed-in rooms. If I don't have to go in a closet or ride a tiny elevator, I'm just a regular old fellow going about his business. It's only when the walls get tight and that door shuts that my brain starts sounding the alarm. Other than that, I'm as steady as they come.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of small spaces related to anything other than the physical characteristics of being enclosed?\n\nPatient: Well, now, let me think on how to put this. Because, you know, I’m not a crazy person. I’ve still got all my marbles, or most of 'em anyway. I know perfectly well that... well, say, a little storage room, like the one where they keep the extra folding chairs down at the VFW hall, or that elevator over at the museum I was telling you about... I know going in there isn't the same as being actually locked up. It's not like the sheriff is throwing me in the county jail and tossing away the key. I know that in my head. I know I'm perfectly safe. \n\nBut... well, it’s like my mind just doesn't care about the facts of the matter. It just treats it like real, serious trouble the absolute minute I see there's no window and that door shuts tight. It's just the physical reality of the room doing it to me. You take away the windows, you close the door, and my brain just decides we're in danger, plain and simple. \n\nI used to tell my wife Martha—before she passed, God rest her soul—I'd say, \"Martha, it makes no sense at all.\" Because there’s no logical reason for it. I wasn't locked in a trunk as a kid or trapped in a cave-in or anything like you see on the television. There's no deep, dark secret behind it. It's just... the minute that door clicks shut and there's no way to look outside, my mind says \"trouble,\" and that's all there is to it. So, no, I don't think it's related to anything else. It's just the box itself.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of small spaces solely because you feel you cannot escape when you are in them?\n\nPatient: Well, now, you’re circling back to that, and... and I appreciate you trying to get to the absolute bottom of it, doc, I really do. But like I was trying to explain a little bit ago, I just don't have a clear-cut answer for you on if it's solely the escaping part. \n\nI mean, I sit and I turn it over in my head, right? I'll be having my morning coffee—I still make it in that old percolator Martha bought at Sears Roebuck back in the seventies, makes a terrible racket on the stove but it brews a darn good cup—and I'll sit there at the kitchen table looking out the window and think, \"Is it really just the fact that I can't get out?\" \n\nBut then I think about the walls themselves, and how the air almost feels a little thicker in a tiny room, and... well, I just end up right back where I started. It’s all a big jumble in my head. I can't rightly say it’s only because I can't escape, because the whole thing just hits me all at once. If there’s some other piece to the puzzle, some other specific reason my brain decides to throw a fit when I'm boxed in, I sure haven't figured it out yet. \n\nI wish I could give you a simple \"yes\" or \"no\" on that one, I really do. It would make this a whole lot easier for the both of us. But at my age, you start to realize some things just don't have a neat little bow on 'em. It’s just the small space, and the door, and the feeling of being trapped, all mixed up together like a big pot of stew. So... I can't tell you it's solely the escaping, but I can't tell you what else it would be, neither. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Is it the inability to escape that triggers your fear, rather than just being in a small space?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, you're asking me to split hairs again, and I... I just don't know if I can pull 'em apart like that. You're asking if it's the escaping part or just the space itself, and... well, it's like trying to figure out if it's the heat or the humidity that makes a Georgia summer so miserable. It's both, ain't it? \n\nI mean, I think about that elevator at the museum. The one with the brass buttons that look like they haven't been polished since the Eisenhower administration. When those doors slide shut, is it the fact that the walls are right up against my elbows, or is it the fact that I couldn't pry those doors open if my life depended on it? \n\nI just... I don't know. My brain doesn't stop to sort it into neat little categories like that. It just sees the walls, and the closed door, and it all hits me at once. It's just the whole kit and caboodle. I wish I could give you a better answer, I really do, but... I just can't say for sure if it's one thing over the other. It's just the whole situation that gets my heart going.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in small, enclosed spaces, or is it also related to the idea of being confined or restricted in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see, there you go again trying to put a fine point on it, and... and I just don't know if I can give you a straight answer on that one either. You're asking if it's just the small space, or if it's the idea of being restricted in general. And... well, I sit and think about it, and it just gets all tangled up in my head.\n\nI mean, I think about when I was younger, working down at the lumber yard. Sometimes I'd have to crawl under the decking to fish out a dropped tool or something, and... well, it was tight under there, real tight. But I don't remember feeling that same panic, not like I do now with that museum elevator. So is it just the small space? I don't know. \n\nBut then I think about being restricted... like, what if I was tied up in a chair in the middle of a big empty gymnasium? Would I feel that same panic? I just... I can't rightly say. I haven't ever been tied to a chair, thank the Lord. The closest I ever came was when my grandson, little Jimmy—he's not so little anymore, just started his first year at the state college, studying business or some such thing—he tied me up with his jump rope when he was playing cowboys and Indians. I just laughed and played along. But that's not the same thing, is it?\n\nSo... I just don't know, doc. I don't know if it's only the small spaces, or if it's the idea of being confined in any way. It's like I said before, it's all just a big jumble. When I'm in that little room, and the door closes, my brain just says \"danger,\" and it doesn't stop to explain why. I wish I could be more helpful, I really do. But I just don't have a good answer for you on that one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or panicked if you were in a large, open space but were somehow restricted from moving around?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see, that's exactly what I was just trying to puzzle out, ain't it? A big open space, but I can't move... I mean, you're really making me stretch my imagination here, doc. \n\nI try to picture it, right? Say I'm standing out in the middle of the high school football field. The one over on 4th Street, where they just put in those new aluminum bleachers last summer. Used to be wood, but you know how the weather rots 'em out. Anyway, say I'm standing right there on the fifty-yard line, wide open sky above me, not a wall in sight. But... I can't move. Maybe my feet are stuck in cement, or... or like I said before, tied up somehow. \n\nWould I panic? Would my heart start doing that fluttery thing against my ribs? I sit here and I try to put myself in that exact spot, and... well, I just don't know. It's awful hard to say how a fella would react to something that's never actually happened to him. \n\nI mean, I don't like the sound of it, not one bit. Being stuck anywhere doesn't sound like a picnic. But would it bring on that same cold sweat I get when I'm looking at the inside of a closet door? I just can't say for sure. My brain keeps trying to compare it to the elevator, or that time I got stuck in the downstairs bathroom when the lock jammed—had to wait three hours for my neighbor, Tom, to come pry the hinges off, and let me tell you, I was a wreck by the time he got there. \n\nBut a big open field? It's just so different. I wish I could give you a solid answer, doc. I know you're trying to draw a line in the sand to see what side I fall on, but I just don't have the experience to tell you one way or the other. I just don't know if it's the being stuck, or the walls, or both. It's just a big question mark for me.\n\nDoctor: If you were in a small room but knew for certain you could escape easily, would you still feel anxious or panicked?\n\nPatient: Well now, doc, that's another one of those tricky ones, ain't it? If I knew for absolute certain I could just push the door open and walk right out... I mean, that sounds fine on paper, doesn't it? But paper ain't real life. \n\nI try to imagine it, right? Say I'm in that little storage room down at the church. The one where they keep the extra folding chairs and the big coffee urns for the potlucks. And say they took the latch right off the door, so it's just swinging free. I know I could push it open with one finger. But... if I'm in there, and that door is closed... I just don't know if my brain cares about the latch. \n\nBecause I look at the walls, and I look at the ceiling, and my mind just starts buzzing. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up in a jail cell or something, but my mind treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It doesn't seem to matter if I have the key in my pocket or if the door's wide open to the hallway. The minute the space feels small and closed off, my chest gets tight, and I start breathing a little faster. \n\nIt's like my body doesn't listen to reason. I could stand there and tell myself, \"Now, Earl, you can walk right out that door anytime you please,\" but my heart's already beating like a drum, and my palms are getting sweaty. So, to answer your question, even if I knew I could escape, I think the panic would still be there, just waiting to pounce. It's the space itself that gets me, not just the lock on the door. It's the whole feeling of being closed in, you know? It's just... it's just awful hard to shake once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small room with an open door?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that kinda goes right back to what I was just rambling about a second ago, doesn't it? If the door is wide open... well, I suppose I'm mostly alright. \n\nLet me give you an example so you see what I mean. We got this pantry off the kitchen at my house. My late wife, Martha—God rest her soul—she made me build it for her back in '82 because she started getting real heavy into canning. Tomatoes, green beans, pickled beets, you name it. Now, it's a tiny little space, barely bigger than a telephone booth. Just shelves on both sides and barely enough room to turn around. And of course, being a pantry, it doesn't have a window. \n\nNow, if I go in there to grab a can of soup or a jar of peaches, and I leave that door swung wide open to the kitchen, I'm fine. I can hear the refrigerator humming out there, I can see the sunlight hitting the linoleum floor, and I don't feel that panic. I'm right as rain. \n\nBut... it's like I was just telling you. I know a little storage room, or my pantry, or that elevator at the museum we talked about... I know it isn't the same as being locked up in the county jail. My logical brain knows I'm just getting some soup. But my mind just treats it like serious trouble the very minute I see there's no window and that door shuts tight. \n\nIf it's open, I got a lifeline, see? The air can get in, I can see the rest of the house. But the second it closes, that's when the switch flips and the walls start creeping in. So, to answer you... if the door stays open, I think I'd be okay. It's the shutting of it that brings on the trouble.\n\nDoctor: Is the panic you feel in small spaces due to the smallness of the space or the feeling of being unable to leave?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I feel like a bit of a broken record here, 'cause I think I keep circling back to the exact same thing. It's awful hard to pull those two things apart—the size of the place and the feeling of being stuck. They just get all tangled up together like a bad ball of fishing line, you know? \n\nLike I was saying just a minute ago... I know a little storage room, or that elevator over at the museum, I know it isn't the same thing as being locked up in solitary confinement. I'm an old man, I've been around the block, and my logical brain knows the difference between a pantry and a prison. But my mind? My mind just throws all that good sense right out the window. It treats the whole situation like serious trouble the absolute minute I see there's no window and that door shuts tight. \n\nSo, to get to the meat of your question... is it the smallness or the leaving? I guess... well, I guess it's the smallness that makes the leaving feel so urgent, if that makes any sense. If I'm in a big room and the door shuts, I got air. I got space to pace around, like that football field we were trying to imagine earlier. But in a small room? Once that door clicks shut, and there's no glass to look out of, the walls just feel like they're sucking up all the oxygen in the room. \n\nIt's like when you're packing away heavy winter coats into a cedar chest, and you press 'em all down in there and shut that heavy lid... that's exactly how my chest feels. So even if the door isn't locked, my brain is already hollering that I'm trapped. I just can't separate the two for you, doc, I really can't. It's the tight space and the shut door working together that brings on the sweat.\n\nDoctor: If you were in a small room with a door that you knew you could open at any time, but someone was standing in front of the door so you couldn't leave, would you feel anxious or panicked?\n\nPatient: Well, now you're adding people into the mix! Let me just picture that for a second. \n\nSay I'm over at the VFW hall—I used to go down there for the Friday fish fries before my stomach started acting up on me with all that grease. They got this tiny little coat closet right off the main entrance. Barely enough room for two people to stand in. Now, say I'm in there grabbing my jacket, and somebody—say, big old Frank Henderson, he was a giant of a man, God rest him—say Frank comes along and stands right smack in the doorway to ask me a question. He's just talking, not meaning any harm, and the door itself is wide open behind him. I know I could just ask him to step aside, or I could squeeze past him if I really had to. \n\nBut doc, I swear I must sound like an old skipping phonograph record today, because it just comes right back down to what I've been telling you all afternoon. But my mind just treats it like terrible trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight—or, in this case, if a person is standing there acting like a shut door. \n\nIf my way out is blocked, the air just gets thick. It doesn't matter if the door is technically unlocked behind him or if I know the fella. If I can't just step out into the open space the very second I want to, my brain starts hollering at me that I'm trapped. My heart starts doing that hammering thing, the sweat starts up on my neck, and I just get this overwhelming urge to push him out of the way and get to the fresh air. So yes sir, if somebody was standing there blocking my way out of a tiny room, I'd be crawling out of my skin just the same. It's the being closed in that does it, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of small spaces due to the smallness of the space or would you feel the same level of anxiety if you were in a large space but unable to leave?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I feel like I'm just chewing on the same piece of gristle here, 'cause I know I've already told you half a dozen times about that museum elevator and the storage rooms. I know I keep saying that my logical brain understands I'm not actually locked up in the county jail, but my mind just hits the panic button the second I see there's no window and a door closes on me. I know I'm repeating myself, but it's just the God's honest truth of how my head works. \n\nBut to really get at what you're asking now—about a big space versus a little one—let me think on that for a second. \n\nSay you put me in the big gymnasium over at the local high school. My grandson, Tommy, he used to play basketball there back in the late nineties, and that place is massive. High ceilings, big bleachers on both sides, echoes when you talk. Now, if I was standing right in the middle of that basketball court and somebody locked all the double doors so I couldn't get out... well, I'd be mad as a wet hen, sure. I'd be pacing around. Nobody likes being told they can't leave a place, and I'd probably be kicking the doors and hollering for the janitor to come let me out. \n\nBut would I have that same awful, suffocating panic? The sweating and the heart pounding? No, sir, I don't believe I would. In a big room like that, I've got all that air above me. I can walk from one end of the court to the other. The walls aren't right up against my nose, pressing in on me. \n\nSo it always comes right back to what I've been harping on. It's those little, tight spaces that do me in. The very minute I'm in a tight spot with no glass to look out of and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like the absolute worst kind of trouble. So yes, it really is the smallness of it all. If the room is big, even if I'm stuck, I can still breathe. But if it's small, that's when I feel like I'm suffocating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, when you really get down to the brass tacks of it, aren’t they sort of two sides of the same old coin? I’ve sat on the porch and chewed on this kind of thing, like I said, but separating the two... I just don't know if I can rightly do that. \n\nYou see, when I look at a small space—like, say, that little half-bath over at my daughter's house, the one she had put in right under the stairs, which is just a toilet and a tiny sink and barely enough room for a man my size to turn around in—I see those walls, right? I see how close they are to my shoulders. And the very next second, my brain says, \"If that door shuts, you can't get out of here easy.\" \n\nSo, is it the walls being close, or is it the not getting out? I honestly couldn't tell you for sure. It’s a bit like asking if it’s the rain that gets you wet or the water in the rain, isn't it? I remember back in... oh, it must have been the winter of '98, we had this terrible ice storm that knocked the power out for nearly a week. Martha and I were cooped up in the living room with just the fireplace going to keep from freezing. We couldn't leave the house because the roads were a solid sheet of ice, completely trapped, but the house itself was big enough, so I didn't feel that panic. I didn't mind being stuck. \n\nBut you put me in a small room... well, they just go hand in hand, don't they? The space is small, which means I'm trapped, and I'm trapped because the space is small. I suppose if the room was small but somehow didn't have any walls, I wouldn't care. But I really just don't know how to give you a straight answer on that one, doc. I haven't been able to separate the two in my head. I haven't really noticed one being worse than the other, it's just... the whole kit and caboodle all at once that makes me want to turn tail and walk the other way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in small, enclosed spaces like elevators and rooms, or are there other situations that trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, like I was sort of touching on a minute ago with the driving and the crowds, I really haven't noticed anything else that gives me that... that particular flutter in my chest. I mean, I go about my day just fine otherwise. \n\nI walk down to the grocery store—used to be a Piggly Wiggly, but they bought it out and turned it into a Food Lion back in, oh, must have been 2006 or 2007—and I can walk down those aisles, even on a Tuesday morning when they're restocking the shelves and it's all cluttered up with cardboard boxes and carts in the way. Doesn't bother me a lick. I just say \"excuse me\" and squeeze right on past. \n\nAnd you know, some folks get spooked by all sorts of everyday things. My neighbor, Earl, he lives two houses down, he can't stand the sight of a snake. Even a little green garter snake in his tomato plants will have him hollering and running for the hills. But me? I'll just scoop it up with a snow shovel and toss it over the fence into the woods. Heights don't bother me neither. I used to clean my own gutters right up until my knees started giving out on me a few years back. My daughter Sarah, she'd come over and yell at me, saying, \"Dad, you're gonna fall and break your neck up there!\" But I was never scared of being up on that ladder, looking down. I just liked keeping the pine needles clear so the water wouldn't back up into the eaves, you know? \n\nSo when I really sit down and take inventory of it all, like I do on the porch... no, I can't rightly say there's anything else. No dogs, no thunderstorms, no heights. Just those little boxed-in rooms. If I don't have to go in a closet or ride a tiny elevator, I'm just a regular old fellow going about his business. It's only when the walls get tight and that door shuts that my brain starts sounding the alarm. Other than that, I'm as steady as they come.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of small spaces related to anything other than the physical characteristics of being enclosed?\n\nPatient: Well, now, let me think on how to put this. Because, you know, I’m not a crazy person. I’ve still got all my marbles, or most of 'em anyway. I know perfectly well that... well, say, a little storage room, like the one where they keep the extra folding chairs down at the VFW hall, or that elevator over at the museum I was telling you about... I know going in there isn't the same as being actually locked up. It's not like the sheriff is throwing me in the county jail and tossing away the key. I know that in my head. I know I'm perfectly safe. \n\nBut... well, it’s like my mind just doesn't care about the facts of the matter. It just treats it like real, serious trouble the absolute minute I see there's no window and that door shuts tight. It's just the physical reality of the room doing it to me. You take away the windows, you close the door, and my brain just decides we're in danger, plain and simple. \n\nI used to tell my wife Martha—before she passed, God rest her soul—I'd say, \"Martha, it makes no sense at all.\" Because there’s no logical reason for it. I wasn't locked in a trunk as a kid or trapped in a cave-in or anything like you see on the television. There's no deep, dark secret behind it. It's just... the minute that door clicks shut and there's no way to look outside, my mind says \"trouble,\" and that's all there is to it. So, no, I don't think it's related to anything else. It's just the box itself.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of small spaces solely because you feel you cannot escape when you are in them?\n\nPatient: Well, now, you’re circling back to that, and... and I appreciate you trying to get to the absolute bottom of it, doc, I really do. But like I was trying to explain a little bit ago, I just don't have a clear-cut answer for you on if it's solely the escaping part. \n\nI mean, I sit and I turn it over in my head, right? I'll be having my morning coffee—I still make it in that old percolator Martha bought at Sears Roebuck back in the seventies, makes a terrible racket on the stove but it brews a darn good cup—and I'll sit there at the kitchen table looking out the window and think, \"Is it really just the fact that I can't get out?\" \n\nBut then I think about the walls themselves, and how the air almost feels a little thicker in a tiny room, and... well, I just end up right back where I started. It’s all a big jumble in my head. I can't rightly say it’s only because I can't escape, because the whole thing just hits me all at once. If there’s some other piece to the puzzle, some other specific reason my brain decides to throw a fit when I'm boxed in, I sure haven't figured it out yet. \n\nI wish I could give you a simple \"yes\" or \"no\" on that one, I really do. It would make this a whole lot easier for the both of us. But at my age, you start to realize some things just don't have a neat little bow on 'em. It’s just the small space, and the door, and the feeling of being trapped, all mixed up together like a big pot of stew. So... I can't tell you it's solely the escaping, but I can't tell you what else it would be, neither. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Is it the inability to escape that triggers your fear, rather than just being in a small space?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, you're asking me to split hairs again, and I... I just don't know if I can pull 'em apart like that. You're asking if it's the escaping part or just the space itself, and... well, it's like trying to figure out if it's the heat or the humidity that makes a Georgia summer so miserable. It's both, ain't it? \n\nI mean, I think about that elevator at the museum. The one with the brass buttons that look like they haven't been polished since the Eisenhower administration. When those doors slide shut, is it the fact that the walls are right up against my elbows, or is it the fact that I couldn't pry those doors open if my life depended on it? \n\nI just... I don't know. My brain doesn't stop to sort it into neat little categories like that. It just sees the walls, and the closed door, and it all hits me at once. It's just the whole kit and caboodle. I wish I could give you a better answer, I really do, but... I just can't say for sure if it's one thing over the other. It's just the whole situation that gets my heart going.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in small, enclosed spaces, or is it also related to the idea of being confined or restricted in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see, there you go again trying to put a fine point on it, and... and I just don't know if I can give you a straight answer on that one either. You're asking if it's just the small space, or if it's the idea of being restricted in general. And... well, I sit and think about it, and it just gets all tangled up in my head.\n\nI mean, I think about when I was younger, working down at the lumber yard. Sometimes I'd have to crawl under the decking to fish out a dropped tool or something, and... well, it was tight under there, real tight. But I don't remember feeling that same panic, not like I do now with that museum elevator. So is it just the small space? I don't know. \n\nBut then I think about being restricted... like, what if I was tied up in a chair in the middle of a big empty gymnasium? Would I feel that same panic? I just... I can't rightly say. I haven't ever been tied to a chair, thank the Lord. The closest I ever came was when my grandson, little Jimmy—he's not so little anymore, just started his first year at the state college, studying business or some such thing—he tied me up with his jump rope when he was playing cowboys and Indians. I just laughed and played along. But that's not the same thing, is it?\n\nSo... I just don't know, doc. I don't know if it's only the small spaces, or if it's the idea of being confined in any way. It's like I said before, it's all just a big jumble. When I'm in that little room, and the door closes, my brain just says \"danger,\" and it doesn't stop to explain why. I wish I could be more helpful, I really do. But I just don't have a good answer for you on that one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or panicked if you were in a large, open space but were somehow restricted from moving around?\n\nPatient: Well, now, see, that's exactly what I was just trying to puzzle out, ain't it? A big open space, but I can't move... I mean, you're really making me stretch my imagination here, doc. \n\nI try to picture it, right? Say I'm standing out in the middle of the high school football field. The one over on 4th Street, where they just put in those new aluminum bleachers last summer. Used to be wood, but you know how the weather rots 'em out. Anyway, say I'm standing right there on the fifty-yard line, wide open sky above me, not a wall in sight. But... I can't move. Maybe my feet are stuck in cement, or... or like I said before, tied up somehow. \n\nWould I panic? Would my heart start doing that fluttery thing against my ribs? I sit here and I try to put myself in that exact spot, and... well, I just don't know. It's awful hard to say how a fella would react to something that's never actually happened to him. \n\nI mean, I don't like the sound of it, not one bit. Being stuck anywhere doesn't sound like a picnic. But would it bring on that same cold sweat I get when I'm looking at the inside of a closet door? I just can't say for sure. My brain keeps trying to compare it to the elevator, or that time I got stuck in the downstairs bathroom when the lock jammed—had to wait three hours for my neighbor, Tom, to come pry the hinges off, and let me tell you, I was a wreck by the time he got there. \n\nBut a big open field? It's just so different. I wish I could give you a solid answer, doc. I know you're trying to draw a line in the sand to see what side I fall on, but I just don't have the experience to tell you one way or the other. I just don't know if it's the being stuck, or the walls, or both. It's just a big question mark for me.\n\nDoctor: If you were in a small room but knew for certain you could escape easily, would you still feel anxious or panicked?\n\nPatient: Well now, doc, that's another one of those tricky ones, ain't it? If I knew for absolute certain I could just push the door open and walk right out... I mean, that sounds fine on paper, doesn't it? But paper ain't real life. \n\nI try to imagine it, right? Say I'm in that little storage room down at the church. The one where they keep the extra folding chairs and the big coffee urns for the potlucks. And say they took the latch right off the door, so it's just swinging free. I know I could push it open with one finger. But... if I'm in there, and that door is closed... I just don't know if my brain cares about the latch. \n\nBecause I look at the walls, and I look at the ceiling, and my mind just starts buzzing. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up in a jail cell or something, but my mind treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It doesn't seem to matter if I have the key in my pocket or if the door's wide open to the hallway. The minute the space feels small and closed off, my chest gets tight, and I start breathing a little faster. \n\nIt's like my body doesn't listen to reason. I could stand there and tell myself, \"Now, Earl, you can walk right out that door anytime you please,\" but my heart's already beating like a drum, and my palms are getting sweaty. So, to answer your question, even if I knew I could escape, I think the panic would still be there, just waiting to pounce. It's the space itself that gets me, not just the lock on the door. It's the whole feeling of being closed in, you know? It's just... it's just awful hard to shake once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small room with an open door?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that kinda goes right back to what I was just rambling about a second ago, doesn't it? If the door is wide open... well, I suppose I'm mostly alright. \n\nLet me give you an example so you see what I mean. We got this pantry off the kitchen at my house. My late wife, Martha—God rest her soul—she made me build it for her back in '82 because she started getting real heavy into canning. Tomatoes, green beans, pickled beets, you name it. Now, it's a tiny little space, barely bigger than a telephone booth. Just shelves on both sides and barely enough room to turn around. And of course, being a pantry, it doesn't have a window. \n\nNow, if I go in there to grab a can of soup or a jar of peaches, and I leave that door swung wide open to the kitchen, I'm fine. I can hear the refrigerator humming out there, I can see the sunlight hitting the linoleum floor, and I don't feel that panic. I'm right as rain. \n\nBut... it's like I was just telling you. I know a little storage room, or my pantry, or that elevator at the museum we talked about... I know it isn't the same as being locked up in the county jail. My logical brain knows I'm just getting some soup. But my mind just treats it like serious trouble the very minute I see there's no window and that door shuts tight. \n\nIf it's open, I got a lifeline, see? The air can get in, I can see the rest of the house. But the second it closes, that's when the switch flips and the walls start creeping in. So, to answer you... if the door stays open, I think I'd be okay. It's the shutting of it that brings on the trouble.\n\nDoctor: Is the panic you feel in small spaces due to the smallness of the space or the feeling of being unable to leave?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I feel like a bit of a broken record here, 'cause I think I keep circling back to the exact same thing. It's awful hard to pull those two things apart—the size of the place and the feeling of being stuck. They just get all tangled up together like a bad ball of fishing line, you know? \n\nLike I was saying just a minute ago... I know a little storage room, or that elevator over at the museum, I know it isn't the same thing as being locked up in solitary confinement. I'm an old man, I've been around the block, and my logical brain knows the difference between a pantry and a prison. But my mind? My mind just throws all that good sense right out the window. It treats the whole situation like serious trouble the absolute minute I see there's no window and that door shuts tight. \n\nSo, to get to the meat of your question... is it the smallness or the leaving? I guess... well, I guess it's the smallness that makes the leaving feel so urgent, if that makes any sense. If I'm in a big room and the door shuts, I got air. I got space to pace around, like that football field we were trying to imagine earlier. But in a small room? Once that door clicks shut, and there's no glass to look out of, the walls just feel like they're sucking up all the oxygen in the room. \n\nIt's like when you're packing away heavy winter coats into a cedar chest, and you press 'em all down in there and shut that heavy lid... that's exactly how my chest feels. So even if the door isn't locked, my brain is already hollering that I'm trapped. I just can't separate the two for you, doc, I really can't. It's the tight space and the shut door working together that brings on the sweat.\n\nDoctor: If you were in a small room with a door that you knew you could open at any time, but someone was standing in front of the door so you couldn't leave, would you feel anxious or panicked?\n\nPatient: Well, now you're adding people into the mix! Let me just picture that for a second. \n\nSay I'm over at the VFW hall—I used to go down there for the Friday fish fries before my stomach started acting up on me with all that grease. They got this tiny little coat closet right off the main entrance. Barely enough room for two people to stand in. Now, say I'm in there grabbing my jacket, and somebody—say, big old Frank Henderson, he was a giant of a man, God rest him—say Frank comes along and stands right smack in the doorway to ask me a question. He's just talking, not meaning any harm, and the door itself is wide open behind him. I know I could just ask him to step aside, or I could squeeze past him if I really had to. \n\nBut doc, I swear I must sound like an old skipping phonograph record today, because it just comes right back down to what I've been telling you all afternoon. I know I've said it a dozen times, but I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up. I know Frank standing there isn't the same as a prison guard. But my mind just treats it like terrible trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight—or, in this case, if a person is standing there acting like a shut door. \n\nIf my way out is blocked, the air just gets thick. It doesn't matter if the door is technically unlocked behind him or if I know the fella. If I can't just step out into the open space the very second I want to, my brain starts hollering at me that I'm trapped. My heart starts doing that hammering thing, the sweat starts up on my neck, and I just get this overwhelming urge to push him out of the way and get to the fresh air. So yes sir, if somebody was standing there blocking my way out of a tiny room, I'd be crawling out of my skin just the same. It's the being closed in that does it, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of small spaces due to the smallness of the space or would you feel the same level of anxiety if you were in a large space but unable to leave?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I feel like I'm just chewing on the same piece of gristle here, 'cause I know I've already told you half a dozen times about that museum elevator and the storage rooms. I know I keep saying that my logical brain understands I'm not actually locked up in the county jail, but my mind just hits the panic button the second I see there's no window and a door closes on me. I know I'm repeating myself, but it's just the God's honest truth of how my head works. \n\nBut to really get at what you're asking now—about a big space versus a little one—let me think on that for a second. \n\nSay you put me in the big gymnasium over at the local high school. My grandson, Tommy, he used to play basketball there back in the late nineties, and that place is massive. High ceilings, big bleachers on both sides, echoes when you talk. Now, if I was standing right in the middle of that basketball court and somebody locked all the double doors so I couldn't get out... well, I'd be mad as a wet hen, sure. I'd be pacing around. Nobody likes being told they can't leave a place, and I'd probably be kicking the doors and hollering for the janitor to come let me out. \n\nBut would I have that same awful, suffocating panic? The sweating and the heart pounding? No, sir, I don't believe I would. In a big room like that, I've got all that air above me. I can walk from one end of the court to the other. The walls aren't right up against my nose, pressing in on me. \n\nSo it always comes right back to what I've been harping on. It's those little, tight spaces that do me in. Even though I know a pantry or a coat closet isn't a prison cell, my brain just doesn't listen to a lick of reason. The very minute I'm in a tight spot with no glass to look out of and that door shuts tight, my mind treats it like the absolute worst kind of trouble. So yes, it really is the smallness of it all. If the room is big, even if I'm stuck, I can still breathe. But if it's small, that's when I feel like I'm suffocating."
  }
]
